It’s just that back then, if I lost a worksheet, I got a frowny face sticker. Now, if I lose an email, I lose a job opportunity. The stakes are just so much higher now, and I don't have my parents or teachers standing over me making sure I actually do the things I'm supposed to do. It’s all on me, and I’m just... I’m really failing at it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned talking out of turn and not being able to hold thoughts in as a kid. Do you still find yourself doing that now—like interrupting people, or having trouble sitting still, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to control?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. Yes, I do. It’s actually—well, honestly, it’s one of the things I hate most about myself right now, especially with the whole job hunt thing I was telling you about. \n\nBecause what happens is, I’ll be in a conversation, or even worse, an actual professional interview, right? And the person will be asking me a question, and like... halfway through their sentence, my brain just decides, 'Oh, I know exactly what they’re asking! I know the end of this sentence!' And I get this overwhelming urge where I feel like I just cannot wait for them to finish talking. So I just jump right in. I totally cut them off. \n\nAnd in my head, I always think I’m being helpful, you know? Like I'm showing that I’m quick and engaged and on the same page as them. But then—and this happens so, so often—I’ll start talking and halfway through my answer I realize, oh no, that is *not* where they were going with that question at all. Like, I completely misjudged it. So not only did I just rudely interrupt them and talk over them, but now I’m rambling about something completely irrelevant, answering the totally wrong question. \n\nIt is so humiliating. I was on a phone screen for this entry-level coordinator role a couple of weeks ago, and the recruiter was asking about my coursework, and I just blurted out this whole long spiel about a specific group project before she even finished her thought. There was this awful, awkward silence on the phone afterward, and I could just tell she was annoyed. It’s like my mouth just goes on autopilot because I can't hold the thought in. I try to tell myself before every single call, 'Just bite your tongue, let them finish,' but in the moment, the thought is just so loud and I have to get it out before I forget it. It just makes me look so unprofessional and scattered, and I don't know how to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down—like right now, or during a class, or at a desk working—do you find yourself fidgeting a lot? I mean, even right now, I don't know if you've noticed, but I've been shifting my weight and messing with my cuticles basically this entire time we've been talking. It’s just... it’s like this literal, physical pressure building up inside my body. When I have to sit through something where I'm just supposed to be still and focus—like an interview, or a meeting, or honestly, even just sitting in a dark theater trying to watch a movie—it gets so incredibly intense. \n\nI actually went to the movies with my friend last week, and everyone else is just glued to the screen, perfectly still, right? But I am sitting there twisting my legs like a pretzel, then untwisting them, then bouncing my heel on the floor, then digging through my purse for a mint just to have something to do. My friend kept giving me the side-eye because I was shaking the shared armrest. It's this trapped energy, and I just *have* to get up, or shift around, or do something with my hands. \n\nAnd it’s so embarrassing during interviews! Or I have a pen just out of frame that I am furiously clicking or twirling, or I'm tearing up a sticky note into tiny little pieces under the desk. I try so hard to look calm and collected, like a normal professional adult who can just sit and have a conversation, but my knee is under the desk bouncing a million miles an hour. But I always just assumed that office was for people who, you know, needed physical things, like wheelchair access or sign language interpreters or things like that. It literally never once crossed my mind that someone could just walk in there and ask for extra time on a test just because they couldn't focus. I mean, can you imagine? I would spend like forty dollars on it, and I’d buy a whole new pack of those nice felt-tip pens in every color of the rainbow, and I would sit down at my desk in my dorm room and tell myself, 'Okay, this is it. This is the semester I get it together. I’m going to write down every single due date, I’m going to color-code my classes, I’m going to review my notes for exactly two hours every night after dinner.' \n\nAnd I would actually do it... for maybe three days? Tops. You would think I could just focus on getting a new position and that would be my only job. But I am just constantly dropping the ball. I’ve had a few interviews, but I have missed so many interview follow-ups. And by then it feels way too late and too awkward, so I just avoid it entirely. It’s costing me actual opportunities, and I know it is, but I just keep doing it.\n\nAnd it’s bleeding into the things I actually enjoy doing, too. It got to the point where I felt like I was actively making things harder for everyone else because they couldn't rely on me. So I actually had to step back from it completely. I just quit. Which really, really hurt, because I genuinely cared about it and it was one of the only things getting me out of the house.\n\nSo, yeah, it is causing massive problems. I'm sitting here unemployed, I'm isolating myself from things I care about, and I just feel like... I don't know, I just feel like I cannot reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward. Like, just the fundamental, everyday things you need to do to be a functioning adult and get a job and be part of a community are completely overwhelming to me right now. I just feel so incredibly stuck in one place.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very early childhood—say, before you were 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with paying attention, organizing things, and needing to move around? Or did those issues start appearing later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Oh, it was definitely way before middle school. Like, right from the very beginning. I actually found a whole box of my old elementary school report cards a couple of months ago when I was supposed to be helping my mom clean out the hall closet—which, of course, turned into me sitting on the floor for two hours reading through every single piece of paper while she did all the actual organizing. But looking at those old report cards, it was honestly wild, because every single teacher from kindergarten through fifth grade basically wrote the exact same paragraph about me. \n\nThey would always start by saying that I was a really bright girl, very enthusiastic, which was nice, I guess, but then it was just a laundry list of all these things I was doing wrong. They all noted that I was constantly losing my worksheets. Like, literally every single day. And the reason I was losing them was because my desk was just an absolute, complete disaster zone. Like I mentioned earlier about my third-grade teacher? The reason she was digging through my desk is because it was so incredibly messy that the top wouldn't even close flat. It was just stuffed with crumpled up papers and broken pencils and old folders, and I literally could not find my assignments when it was time to turn them in. I would just sit there panicking, pulling out handfuls of trash while everyone else was passing their papers to the front. \n\nAnd the teachers also always wrote that I rushed through directions. And the talking... oh my gosh, the talking. I was always, always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just could not hold it in. If a thought popped into my head, I would just blurt it out immediately, right in the middle of a lesson, or I'd turn around and start talking to the kid behind me while the teacher was still speaking. I just couldn't seem to make myself wait. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't just start when I got older. It was all happening right there in elementary school. It's this trapped energy, and I just *have* to get up, or shift around, or do something with my hands. \n\nAnd it’s so embarrassing during interviews! Like, going back to what I was saying before about the job hunt... not only am I interrupting people, but I’m also sitting there in front of the webcam—because almost all my interviews are on Zoom right now, which is a whole other nightmare because you can see yourself fidgeting on the screen—and I'm constantly adjusting my chair. Or I have a pen just out of frame that I am furiously clicking or twirling, or I'm tearing up a sticky note into tiny little pieces under the desk. I try so hard to look calm and collected, like a normal professional adult who can just sit and have a conversation, but my knee is under the desk bouncing a million miles an hour. It’s exhausting, honestly, just trying to contain it all. It’s just this constant, buzzing need to move, and if I don't give in to it at least a little bit, I can't even hear what the other person is saying because all I can think about is how badly I need to move.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in high school and college, did these issues with attention, organization, and fidgeting continue to cause you problems academically? Like, were you struggling with grades, missing deadlines on assignments, or having trouble in classes despite being bright?\n\nPatient: I mean... I really don't know? I'm honestly not sure if I was missing deadlines or struggling with my grades, specifically. Like, when I try to think back to high school and college, I don't really remember it being like that, or at least, I haven't really noticed a pattern of that when I look back. \n\nHigh school is kind of this huge blur for me, to be completely honest. Because, well, my parents were just so incredibly involved—maybe over-involved, if I'm being real. They were constantly logging into that online parent portal thing... I think it was called PowerSchool? Or maybe it was Canvas? Whatever it was, my mom had the password saved on her iPad and she would refresh it like it was her social media feed. So I don't really know if I would have missed deadlines on my own, because she was basically acting as my external brain. She would literally stand in the doorway of my bedroom while I was just, I don't know, staring at the ceiling or twirling my hair, and she'd interrogate me about every single syllabus. \"Did you submit the history paper? Did you do the math worksheet?\" So I didn't really have the chance to fail classes or see my grades drop, you know? I don't really have this memory of struggling academically because they just wouldn't let me. It drove me absolutely crazy at the time, feeling like I was being micromanaged, but they were always right there.\n\nAnd then in college... I don't know, I just don't have a clear memory of struggling in that specific way. I mean, I got my bachelor's degree, and I'm out here applying for all these entry-level jobs now, so I must have gotten through it! But if you're asking if I was constantly getting bad grades or having trouble passing classes... I just don't really know. I can't say that I did. It’s funny how memory works, right? Like, I can remember exactly how my third-grade teacher looked when she was digging through the trash in my desk, but college deadlines? My mind just goes completely blank. I just don't really have any specific memories of that being a huge problem. I don't know if that makes sense, but yeah, I just really couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: In college, did you have any formal accommodations or support—like extended test time, a note-taker, or anything like that? Or did you develop any particular strategies or routines that helped you stay on track with assignments and studying?\n\nPatient: Accommodations? Oh, um, no. No, definitely not. I mean, I didn't even know that was a thing you could do for... well, for whatever is going on with me. Like, I knew there was a disability services office on campus, right? It was actually in the basement of the student union building, right next to this little café where I used to get iced lattes literally every morning before my 9 AM sociology lecture—which I was almost always late to, by the way, because the line was always ridiculously long and I could never time it right. But I always just assumed that office was for people who, you know, needed physical things, like wheelchair access or sign language interpreters or things like that. It literally never once crossed my mind that someone could just walk in there and ask for extra time on a test just because they couldn't focus. I mean, can you imagine? If I had known that was an option, maybe things would have felt a little less chaotic, but no, I never had a note-taker or extra time or anything official like that at all. \n\nAnd as for strategies or routines... gosh, I don't know. I really don't think I had any? Like, what even counts as a strategy? If you mean, did I have a set study schedule and a designated study spot, then absolutely not. I mean, I *tried*. Oh my gosh, I tried so many times. Every single semester, usually during syllabus week when I was feeling super optimistic, I would go to Target and buy one of those really expensive, beautiful planners—you know, the ones with the thick paper and the inspirational quotes on every page and the little sheets of gold foil stickers in the back? I would spend like forty dollars on it, and I’d buy a whole new pack of those nice felt-tip pens in every color of the rainbow, and I would sit down at my desk in my dorm room and tell myself, 'Okay, this is it. This is the semester I get it together. I’m going to write down every single due date, I’m going to color-code my classes, I’m going to review my notes for exactly two hours every night after dinner.' \n\nAnd I would actually do it... for maybe three days? Tops. And then I would just stop opening it, or I’d shove it under a pile of laundry on my desk and completely forget it even existed until I was packing up my room to move out in May and I'd find it completely blank after January 15th. \n\nSo yeah, I wouldn't say I had any actual routines or strategies that actually worked. I was mostly just flying by the seat of my pants the entire four years. I don't even really know how I got through it, honestly. It was just a lot of panic, honestly, just pure panic and adrenaline right before something was due. So, no, no formal support and definitely no real routines that I can think of. I just kind of winged it the whole time.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these problems with forgetfulness, interrupting, and fidgeting have been going on since elementary school. But thinking about it more broadly—over the past 6 months or so, would you say these issues have been causing you real problems in your daily life, work, or relationships? Or have they pretty much always been at this level of difficulty?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely worse now, or at least the consequences are so much heavier than they used to be. Like, over the past six months, it’s just been a complete disaster, honestly. Because since getting fired—which I know I briefly mentioned earlier, but that whole experience was just so incredibly humiliating because I really did try so hard, I just couldn't keep all the details straight—since that happened, my life has just felt completely off the rails. \n\nSo now I'm unemployed, and I have all the time in the world, right? You would think I could just focus on getting a new position and that would be my only job. But I am just constantly dropping the ball. I’ve had a few interviews, but I have missed so many interview follow-ups. I will literally sit at my laptop, fully intending to send a simple thank-you email, and then I'll open a new tab to check the spelling of the interviewer's name, and then I'll fall down a rabbit hole looking at their LinkedIn, and then I'll go get a snack, and suddenly it's three days later and the draft is just sitting there, unsent. And by then it feels way too late and too awkward, so I just avoid it entirely. It’s costing me actual opportunities, and I know it is, but I just keep doing it.\n\nAnd it’s bleeding into the things I actually enjoy doing, too. I had this volunteer project that I cared about so, so much. We were organizing these community supply drives, and I loved the cause, I loved the people... but there were all these moving parts. Group chats, email chains, coordinating drop-off times. And I was constantly forgetting to reply to the organizers, or I'd interrupt the main coordinator during our planning meetings because my brain was just going too fast and I couldn't hold my thoughts in, or I'd completely blank on a deadline for picking up the donation boxes. It got to the point where I felt like I was actively making things harder for everyone else because they couldn't rely on me. So I actually had to step back from it completely. I just quit. Which really, really hurt, because I genuinely cared about it and it was one of the only things getting me out of the house.\n\nSo, yeah, it is causing massive problems. I'm sitting here unemployed, I'm isolating myself from things I care about, and I just feel like... I don't know, I just feel like I cannot reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward. Like, just the fundamental, everyday things you need to do to be a functioning adult and get a job and be part of a community are completely overwhelming to me right now. I just feel so incredibly stuck in one place.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very early childhood—say, before you were 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with paying attention, organizing things, and needing to move around? Or did those issues start appearing later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Oh, it was definitely way before middle school. Like, right from the very beginning. I actually found a whole box of my old elementary school report cards a couple of months ago when I was supposed to be helping my mom clean out the hall closet—which, of course, turned into me sitting on the floor for two hours reading through every single piece of paper while she did all the actual organizing. But looking at those old report cards, it was honestly wild, because every single teacher from kindergarten through fifth grade basically wrote the exact same paragraph about me. \n\nThey would always start by saying that I was a really bright girl, very enthusiastic, which was nice, I guess, but then it was just a laundry list of all these things I was doing wrong. They all noted that I was constantly losing my worksheets. Like, literally every single day. And the reason I was losing them was because my desk was just an absolute, complete disaster zone. Like I mentioned earlier about my third-grade teacher? The reason she was digging through my desk is because it was so incredibly messy that the top wouldn't even close flat. It was just stuffed with crumpled up papers and broken pencils and old folders, and I literally could not find my assignments when it was time to turn them in. I would just sit there panicking, pulling out handfuls of trash while everyone else was passing their papers to the front. \n\nAnd the teachers also always wrote that I rushed through directions. I would just glance at the top of a page, assume I knew exactly what the whole thing was about, and just speed through it as fast as humanly possible, completely missing what we were actually supposed to do. And the talking... oh my gosh, the talking. I was always, always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just could not hold it in. If a thought popped into my head, I would just blurt it out immediately, right in the middle of a lesson, or I'd turn around and start talking to the kid behind me while the teacher was still speaking. I just couldn't seem to make myself wait. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't just start when I got older. It was all happening right there in elementary school. It's actually kind of frustrating to look back at those report cards now and see it all spelled out so clearly, year after year, just all these notes about me being bright but completely disorganized and disruptive. But I guess back then everyone just thought I was a messy, chatty kid, so it just kept going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Um, yeah, like I was mentioning just a minute ago with that box of report cards I found—it was absolutely right there from the very beginning. Like, kindergarten, first grade, second grade, it was all happening then. There wasn't a delay at all. 

Even in those really early elementary school years, the teachers were already writing all of that down. They would always start off nice, you know, writing that I was a bright kid, which my mom obviously loved to focus on. But then the rest of the evaluation would just be this long, repetitive list of all the exact same issues. They noted that I was constantly losing my worksheets. Just, every single day, something was missing. And it wasn't because I was throwing them away on purpose, it was just because my desk was so incredibly messy that I literally could not find my assignments when it was time to hand them in. I would just shove everything inside—crayons, half-eaten snacks, crumpled up spelling sheets—and it just became this terrifying black hole. 

And then when I actually did have the paper in front of me, I always rushed through the directions. Even when I was six or seven years old, I’d just take one quick glance at the top of the page, assume I completely understood what the whole activity was, and just fly through it as fast as I possibly could. I'd end up doing the whole thing wrong because I didn't actually read what we were supposed to do. 

And, oh, the talking out of turn. That was there from day one, too. If we were sitting on the little carpet squares for storytime, I just could not sit quietly and listen. I had to blurt out whatever popped into my head the exact second I thought of it, right over the teacher or whoever else was talking. So, yeah, it definitely wasn't something that developed later on in middle school or anything. It was just... boom, right out of the gate in kindergarten, all those same things were already happening.

I think it was called PowerSchool? Or maybe it was Canvas? Whatever it was, my mom had the password saved on her iPad and she would refresh it like it was her social media feed. Did you do the math worksheet?\" So I didn't really have the chance to fail classes or see my grades drop, you know? I don't know, I just don't have a clear memory of struggling in that specific way. I mean, I got my bachelor's degree, and I'm out here applying for all these entry-level jobs now, so I must have gotten through it! I just don't really have any specific memories of that being a huge problem. I don't know if that makes sense, but yeah, I just really couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: In college, did you have any formal accommodations or support—like extended test time, a note-taker, or anything like that? It literally never once crossed my mind that someone could just walk in there and ask for extra time on a test just because they couldn't focus. I mean, can you imagine? I would spend like forty dollars on it, and I’d buy a whole new pack of those nice felt-tip pens in every color of the rainbow, and I would sit down at my desk in my dorm room and tell myself, 'Okay, this is it. I was mostly just flying by the seat of my pants the entire four years. I don't even really know how I got through it, honestly. It was just a lot of panic, honestly, just pure panic and adrenaline right before something was due. So, no, no formal support and definitely no real routines that I can think of. I just kind of winged it the whole time.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these problems with forgetfulness, interrupting, and fidgeting have been going on since elementary school. But thinking about it more broadly—over the past 6 months or so, would you say these issues have been causing you real problems in your daily life, work, or relationships? Or have they pretty much always been at this level of difficulty?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely worse now, or at least the consequences are so much heavier than they used to be. Like, over the past six months, it’s just been a complete disaster, honestly. Because since getting fired—which I know I briefly mentioned earlier, but that whole experience was just so incredibly humiliating because I really did try so hard, I just couldn't keep all the details straight—since that happened, my life has just felt completely off the rails. \n\nSo now I'm unemployed, and I have all the time in the world, right? You would think I could just focus on getting a new position and that would be my only job. But I am just constantly dropping the ball. I’ve had a few interviews, but I have missed so many interview follow-ups. I will literally sit at my laptop, fully intending to send a simple thank-you email, and then I'll open a new tab to check the spelling of the interviewer's name, and then I'll fall down a rabbit hole looking at their LinkedIn, and then I'll go get a snack, and suddenly it's three days later and the draft is just sitting there, unsent. And by then it feels way too late and too awkward, so I just avoid it entirely. It’s costing me actual opportunities, and I know it is, but I just keep doing it.\n\nAnd it’s bleeding into the things I actually enjoy doing, too. I had this volunteer project that I cared about so, so much. We were organizing these community supply drives, and I loved the cause, I loved the people... but there were all these moving parts. Group chats, email chains, coordinating drop-off times. And I was constantly forgetting to reply to the organizers, or I'd interrupt the main coordinator during our planning meetings because my brain was just going too fast and I couldn't hold my thoughts in, or I'd completely blank on a deadline for picking up the donation boxes. It got to the point where I felt like I was actively making things harder for everyone else because they couldn't rely on me. So I actually had to step back from it completely. I just quit. Which really, really hurt, because I genuinely cared about it and it was one of the only things getting me out of the house.\n\nSo, yeah, it is causing massive problems. I'm sitting here unemployed, I'm isolating myself from things I care about, and I just feel like... I don't know, I just feel like I cannot reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward. Like, just the fundamental, everyday things you need to do to be a functioning adult and get a job and be part of a community are completely overwhelming to me right now. I just feel so incredibly stuck in one place.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very early childhood—say, before you were 12 years old—do you remember having these same kinds of problems with paying attention, organizing things, and needing to move around? Or did those issues start appearing later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Oh, it was definitely way before middle school. Like, right from the very beginning. I actually found a whole box of my old elementary school report cards a couple of months ago when I was supposed to be helping my mom clean out the hall closet—which, of course, turned into me sitting on the floor for two hours reading through every single piece of paper while she did all the actual organizing. But looking at those old report cards, it was honestly wild, because every single teacher from kindergarten through fifth grade basically wrote the exact same paragraph about me. \n\nThey would always start by saying that I was a really bright girl, very enthusiastic, which was nice, I guess, but then it was just a laundry list of all these things I was doing wrong. They all noted that I was constantly losing my worksheets. Like, literally every single day. And the reason I was losing them was because my desk was just an absolute, complete disaster zone. Like I mentioned earlier about my third-grade teacher? The reason she was digging through my desk is because it was so incredibly messy that the top wouldn't even close flat. It was just stuffed with crumpled up papers and broken pencils and old folders, and I literally could not find my assignments when it was time to turn them in. I would just sit there panicking, pulling out handfuls of trash while everyone else was passing their papers to the front. \n\nAnd the teachers also always wrote that I rushed through directions. I would just glance at the top of a page, assume I knew exactly what the whole thing was about, and just speed through it as fast as humanly possible, completely missing what we were actually supposed to do. And the talking... oh my gosh, the talking. I was always, always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just could not hold it in. If a thought popped into my head, I would just blurt it out immediately, right in the middle of a lesson, or I'd turn around and start talking to the kid behind me while the teacher was still speaking. I just couldn't seem to make myself wait. \n\nSo, yeah, it definitely didn't just start when I got older. It was all happening right there in elementary school. It's actually kind of frustrating to look back at those report cards now and see it all spelled out so clearly, year after year, just all these notes about me being bright but completely disorganized and disruptive. But I guess back then everyone just thought I was a messy, chatty kid, so it just kept going.\n\nDoctor: Just to be clear about the timing—were you experiencing these problems with attention, organization, and fidgeting consistently throughout your entire elementary school years, or do you remember them starting at a particular point, like when you were in a specific grade?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, as far as elementary school goes, it was just... always. Like, there wasn't a specific day in third grade where a switch flipped, you know? It was just a constant from kindergarten all the way through. But, um, I feel like I need to clarify, because back then, it was just... I was just a messy kid, and the stakes were so low. If I forgot something, my mom or a teacher would just fix it for me. \n\nBut if we're talking about when it became this massive, unmanageable thing that is actually ruining my life? That level of it has been seriously affecting me for about a year and two months now. Yeah, about a year and two months of just absolute chaos. Because that's exactly when things started completely falling apart at my biotech job. I was so proud to get that position right out of school, and I really thought, 'Okay, this is it, I'm going to be a real, functioning professional.' But the demands just kept piling up, and all those little mistakes and forgotten details just snowballed. It was this agonizing period of watching myself fail in slow motion, knowing I was messing up but not being able to stop it. \n\nAnd then, of course, I got fired. And you'd think hitting rock bottom like that would be a wake-up call and I'd just magically get my act together, but no, it's just continued entirely after I was let go. This whole past year and two months has just been me stuck in that exact same level of dysfunction, completely unable to get back on track or even send a basic email. So, yeah, the childhood stuff was always there in the background, but this severe, completely-derailing-my-life phase? That's been a solid year and two months.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at those early elementary school years—kindergarten through maybe second or third grade—do you have any memories of not being able to pay attention or sit still, or is that something that started happening a bit later on?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, like I was mentioning just a minute ago with that box of report cards I found—it was absolutely right there from the very beginning. Like, kindergarten, first grade, second grade, it was all happening then. There wasn't a delay at all. \n\nEven in those really early elementary school years, the teachers were already writing all of that down. They would always start off nice, you know, writing that I was a bright kid, which my mom obviously loved to focus on. But then the rest of the evaluation would just be this long, repetitive list of all the exact same issues. They noted that I was constantly losing my worksheets. Just, every single day, something was missing. And it wasn't because I was throwing them away on purpose, it was just because my desk was so incredibly messy that I literally could not find my assignments when it was time to hand them in. I would just shove everything inside—crayons, half-eaten snacks, crumpled up spelling sheets—and it just became this terrifying black hole. \n\nAnd then when I actually did have the paper in front of me, I always rushed through the directions. Even when I was six or seven years old, I’d just take one quick glance at the top of the page, assume I completely understood what the whole activity was, and just fly through it as fast as I possibly could. I'd end up doing the whole thing wrong because I didn't actually read what we were supposed to do. \n\nAnd, oh, the talking out of turn. That was there from day one, too. If we were sitting on the little carpet squares for storytime, I just could not sit quietly and listen. I had to blurt out whatever popped into my head the exact second I thought of it, right over the teacher or whoever else was talking. So, yeah, it definitely wasn't something that developed later on in middle school or anything. It was just... boom, right out of the gate in kindergarten, all those same things were already happening."
